# Item
## id
CON-NEU-B311DF136F1301
## label
The lacrimal nerve (V1's most lateral branch) supplies skin of the upper lateral eyelid
## canonical_key
lacrimal-nerve-upper-eyelid-skin
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The lacrimal nerve is the most lateral of the three branches of the ophthalmic division (V1) of the trigeminal nerve. It runs along the upper border of lateral rectus, through the lacrimal gland, and its terminal cutaneous twigs supply the skin of the lateral part of the upper eyelid and adjoining conjunctiva -- distinct territory from the more medial supraorbital and supratrochlear (frontal nerve) branches, which cover the middle/medial upper eyelid and forehead.
## explicit_objective
Localise the lacrimal nerve's cutaneous territory to the lateral upper eyelid, distinguishing it from the supraorbital/supratrochlear (medial/central) and zygomaticofacial (cheek) territories.
## pitfalls
Assuming any V1 branch covers the whole upper eyelid uniformly -- the three V1 cutaneous branches (lacrimal, supraorbital, supratrochlear) split the upper eyelid/forehead by position, lateral to medial.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-AFE75BD9FFF1F1
## label
Hypoglossal nerve injury paralyses ipsilateral genioglossus, deviating the protruded tongue toward the injured side
## canonical_key
hypoglossal-nerve-tongue-deviation-injured-side
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The hypoglossal nerve (CN XII) loops through the floor of the submandibular triangle on its way to the tongue, making it the named structure most exposed to a penetrating neck wound there. On protrusion, each side's genioglossus normally pulls the tongue forward and slightly to the opposite side, so healthy bilateral action keeps protrusion straight. When one hypoglossal nerve is injured, the unopposed healthy-side genioglossus pushes the tongue toward the weak (injured) side, so the direction of deviation on protrusion localises the lesion to that same side's hypoglossal nerve.
## explicit_objective
State that hypoglossal nerve injury deviates the protruded tongue toward the injured (weak) side, and use this to localise a lesion from the observed direction of deviation.
## pitfalls
Concluding the tongue deviates AWAY from the injured side -- it is the unopposed healthy-side genioglossus that wins, pushing the tongue toward the weak side, not away from it.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-68DB4BD90F1AB1
## label
The facial nerve exits the skull through the stylomastoid foramen before entering the parotid gland
## canonical_key
facial-nerve-stylomastoid-foramen
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
After leaving the brainstem and traversing the facial canal within the temporal bone, the facial nerve (CN VII) exits the skull through the stylomastoid foramen, immediately posterior to the styloid process. It then enters the parotid gland and divides into five terminal branches (temporal, zygomatic, buccal, marginal mandibular, cervical); the marginal mandibular branch supplies only the lower lip muscles (not the upper lip, which is buccal/zygomatic territory), and the facial nerve also supplies the posterior (not anterior) belly of digastric.
## explicit_objective
State that the facial nerve exits the skull via the stylomastoid foramen, giving five terminal branches within the parotid gland, and supplies the posterior belly of digastric.
## pitfalls
Confusing the facial nerve's five parotid branches with four, or crediting it with the anterior belly of digastric (that belongs to the mylohyoid nerve, CN V3) or the whole lip (marginal mandibular is lower lip only).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-EAE11A611DBFBA
## label
The abducent nerve supplies only lateral rectus, producing pure horizontal abduction
## canonical_key
abducent-nerve-lateral-rectus
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The abducent nerve (CN VI) supplies a single extraocular muscle, lateral rectus, which abducts the eye. Because only one muscle is involved, an isolated abducent nerve palsy produces a pure horizontal deviation -- a medially deviated, non-abducting eye -- without affecting elevation, depression or eyelid position, unlike an oculomotor (CN III) palsy which affects multiple muscles and the lid.
## explicit_objective
State that the abducent nerve supplies lateral rectus only, and predict the resulting pure horizontal deficit of an abducent nerve palsy.
## pitfalls
Attributing superior rectus, inferior oblique or the superior tarsal muscle to the abducent nerve -- these are oculomotor (CN III) or sympathetic territory, not abducent.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-18214887A46098
## label
Lateral pterygoid is the sole jaw-opening muscle among the four muscles of mastication
## canonical_key
lateral-pterygoid-jaw-opening
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Of the four muscles of mastication (masseter, temporalis, medial pterygoid, lateral pterygoid), lateral pterygoid alone opens (depresses) the mandible, by pulling the condyle and articular disc forward as the mouth opens. The other three (masseter, temporalis, medial pterygoid) all elevate/close the jaw. A lateral pterygoid palsy classically deviates the jaw TOWARD the weak side on opening, because the healthy contralateral muscle pulls the mandible over unopposed.
## explicit_objective
Identify lateral pterygoid as the jaw-opening muscle of mastication, and predict jaw deviation toward the weak side on opening if it is paralysed.
## pitfalls
Assuming any muscle of mastication opens the jaw -- three of the four (masseter, temporalis, medial pterygoid) are elevators/closers; only lateral pterygoid opens it.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-9B6A273680EB40
## label
The ansa cervicalis's inferior root (descendens cervicalis) arises from C2 and C3
## canonical_key
ansa-cervicalis-inferior-root
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The ansa cervicalis is a nerve loop embedded in the anterior wall of the carotid sheath, formed by two roots: a superior root (descendens hypoglossi), a C1 branch that joins and travels with the hypoglossal nerve before descending independently, and an inferior root (descendens cervicalis), which arises directly from the C2 and C3 anterior rami of the cervical plexus and descends to join the superior root. The completed loop supplies the infrahyoid strap muscles (sternohyoid, sternothyroid, inferior belly of omohyoid), while superior belly omohyoid is supplied separately by the superior root itself.
## explicit_objective
State that the ansa cervicalis's inferior root arises from C2-C3, distinguishing it from the superior root's C1-via-hypoglossal origin.
## pitfalls
Confusing which root is which -- the SUPERIOR root travels with the hypoglossal nerve (C1 fibres), while the INFERIOR root (C2-C3) arises independently from the cervical plexus.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-1DC50D1F3DB730
## label
Omohyoid's inferior belly subdivides the posterior triangle into occipital and subclavian triangles
## canonical_key
posterior-triangle-omohyoid-division
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The inferior belly of omohyoid crosses the posterior triangle of the neck and divides it into a larger occipital triangle above and a smaller, more inferior subclavian (supraclavicular) triangle below. The subclavian triangle is the surgically important subdivision, where the subclavian artery and the trunks of the brachial plexus are accessed.
## explicit_objective
State that omohyoid's inferior belly subdivides the posterior triangle into the occipital and subclavian triangles.
## pitfalls
Crediting trapezius or digastric with subdividing the posterior triangle -- trapezius only bounds it posteriorly, and digastric belongs to the anterior triangle's subdivisions.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-8ACC73AD5ACC96
## label
The maxillary sinus drains via the hiatus semilunaris in the middle meatus
## canonical_key
maxillary-sinus-hiatus-middle-meatus
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The maxillary sinus drains through its ostium into the hiatus semilunaris, a groove on the lateral nasal wall located within the middle meatus -- the same region receiving the frontal sinus and anterior ethmoidal air cells. A tumour or polyp obstructing the middle meatus can therefore block outflow from several sinuses at once, not the maxillary sinus alone.
## explicit_objective
Localise the maxillary sinus's drainage ostium (hiatus semilunaris) to the middle meatus of the lateral nasal wall.
## pitfalls
Placing the maxillary sinus ostium in the inferior meatus (nasolacrimal duct territory) or superior meatus (posterior ethmoidal territory) instead of the middle meatus.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-EC9CFE6447634F
## label
The cavernous sinus lies adjacent to the pituitary gland and is the classic site of septic dural sinus thrombosis
## canonical_key
cavernous-sinus-thrombosis
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The cavernous sinus lies directly lateral to the pituitary gland and sphenoid sinus, making it the dural venous sinus closest to the pituitary. It is a well-known site of septic thrombosis (cavernous sinus thrombosis), because it receives the ophthalmic veins and drains the facial venous plexus, allowing infection to spread there directly from a facial or orbital source via these valveless venous connections.
## explicit_objective
Identify the cavernous sinus as the dural venous sinus adjacent to the pituitary gland and the classic site of septic sinus thrombosis spreading from the face/orbit.
## pitfalls
Confusing the cavernous sinus with the straight, superior petrosal or sigmoid sinuses, none of which sit adjacent to the pituitary fossa.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-41A4186164A486
## label
Mylohyoid forms the muscular diaphragm of the oral cavity (floor of the mouth)
## canonical_key
mylohyoid-oral-diaphragm
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Mylohyoid is a paired, flat muscle whose fibres meet in a median raphe, forming a muscular sling literally termed the diaphragm of the oral cavity -- the floor of the mouth supporting the tongue and submandibular structures above it. Its median raphe and lateral attachment along the mylohyoid line of the mandible give it this sling-like shape; geniohyoid lies deep to it and digastric/stylohyoid instead bound adjacent triangles rather than forming the floor sheet itself.
## explicit_objective
Identify mylohyoid as the muscular diaphragm of the oral cavity (floor of the mouth).
## pitfalls
Crediting geniohyoid, digastric or stylohyoid with forming the floor of the mouth -- only mylohyoid's paired sheet does this; the others sit above it or bound neighbouring triangles.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-E3569CD3646647
## label
Little's area (Kiesselbach's plexus) on the anterior nasal septum is the commonest site of epistaxis
## canonical_key
littles-area-nasal-septum
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Little's area is a vascular plexus (Kiesselbach's plexus) on the anterior part of the nasal septum, where branches of the anterior ethmoidal, sphenopalatine, greater palatine and superior labial arteries anastomose superficially. Because these vessels sit just beneath the septal mucosa with little protective tissue, even minor trauma (nose-picking, dry air, a blow) readily ruptures them, making this the commonest site of epistaxis (nosebleed).
## explicit_objective
Localise Little's area (Kiesselbach's plexus), the commonest site of epistaxis, to the anterior nasal septum.
## pitfalls
Placing Little's area on the lateral wall, roof or floor of the nose instead of the anterior septum.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-9AC902A20DF60E
## label
Ascending pharyngeal artery is a branch of the external carotid artery
## canonical_key
carotid-artery-ascending-pharyngeal-branch
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The ascending pharyngeal artery is a named branch of the external carotid artery -- its smallest and most posteromedial branch -- supplying the pharyngeal wall, prevertebral muscles and part of the middle ear/meninges. The external carotid's other named branches (superior thyroid, lingual, facial, occipital, posterior auricular, superficial temporal, maxillary) are the usual distractors for this fact; the inferior thyroid, internal thoracic and superficial cervical arteries instead arise from the subclavian artery (directly or via the thyrocervical trunk), not the carotid.
## explicit_objective
Identify the ascending pharyngeal artery as an external carotid branch, distinguishing it from subclavian/thyrocervical-trunk branches.
## pitfalls
Attributing inferior thyroid, internal thoracic or superficial cervical arteries to the carotid artery -- all three arise from the subclavian artery/thyrocervical trunk instead.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-F9C251C2830DF4
## label
The middle meningeal artery is a branch of the maxillary artery, entering the skull via foramen spinosum
## canonical_key
middle-meningeal-artery-origin
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The middle meningeal artery arises from the maxillary artery (the largest terminal branch of the external carotid artery), entering the skull through the foramen spinosum to supply the dura mater and calvaria. This course through bone rather than soft tissue is why a skull fracture crossing the pterion can lacerate the artery and produce a rapidly expanding extradural (epidural) haematoma.
## explicit_objective
State that the middle meningeal artery is a branch of the maxillary artery entering the skull via foramen spinosum, and its role in extradural haemorrhage.
## pitfalls
Attributing the middle meningeal artery to the internal carotid or middle cerebral artery -- it is an external carotid-system (maxillary artery) branch, not an intracranial one.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-42ACC071FA7F7C
## label
Tensor veli palatini is the sole palate muscle supplied by the trigeminal nerve, not the pharyngeal plexus
## canonical_key
tensor-palati-trigeminal-innervation
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Every palate muscle except one is supplied by the pharyngeal plexus (vagal fibres, CN X): levator veli palatini, palatoglossus, palatopharyngeus and musculus uvulae. Tensor veli palatini is the sole exception, supplied instead by the mandibular division of the trigeminal nerve (CN V3, via the nerve to medial pterygoid) -- a favourite exam distinction because every other palate muscle breaks the same rule, making tensor veli palatini easy to test as the odd one out.
## explicit_objective
Identify tensor veli palatini as the one palate muscle supplied by the trigeminal nerve (V3), all others being pharyngeal-plexus (vagal).
## pitfalls
Assuming all palate muscles share one nerve supply -- tensor veli palatini alone is trigeminal; the rest are pharyngeal plexus.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-9A6C87C6801DEE
## label
The glossopharyngeal nerve supplies both sensation and taste to the posterior third of the tongue, and is at risk during tonsillectomy
## canonical_key
glossopharyngeal-nerve-posterior-tongue-taste
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The glossopharyngeal nerve (CN IX) runs close to the tonsillar bed -- the tonsillar fossa lies against the pharyngeal wall the nerve crosses -- making it the nerve classically at risk during tonsillectomy. It supplies both general sensation AND taste to the posterior one-third of the tongue, so its injury selectively abolishes taste there while sparing the anterior two-thirds, which is chorda tympani/lingual (facial/trigeminal) nerve territory instead. Because both modalities travel together in CN IX, a single nerve injury explains isolated posterior-tongue taste loss without needing a second nerve.
## explicit_objective
Recognise glossopharyngeal nerve injury during tonsillectomy as a cause of isolated posterior-tongue taste loss, distinguishing its territory from the lingual/chorda tympani's anterior two-thirds.
## pitfalls
Attributing posterior-tongue taste to the lingual nerve or chorda tympani -- those supply only the anterior two-thirds; the posterior third is glossopharyngeal territory for both sensation and taste.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-FD2F4AA203121B
## label
The deep cervical lymph node chain runs alongside the internal jugular vein
## canonical_key
deep-cervical-lymph-nodes-internal-jugular
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The deep cervical lymph nodes form a chain running the length of the neck alongside the internal jugular vein, within the carotid sheath, receiving drainage from the superficial cervical nodes and from most head-and-neck structures before draining into the jugular lymphatic trunk. This is distinct from the superficial cervical nodes, which run alongside the external jugular vein instead.
## explicit_objective
State that the deep cervical lymph nodes lie alongside the internal jugular vein, distinguishing them from the superficial chain along the external jugular vein.
## pitfalls
Confusing the deep chain (internal jugular vein) with the superficial chain (external jugular vein), or with the subclavian/anterior jugular veins, which are not the nodal landmark vessel for either chain.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-5570AF5F9F0CF3
## label
Superior constrictor arises in part from the pterygomandibular raphe
## canonical_key
superior-constrictor-pterygomandibular-raphe
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The superior constrictor of the pharynx arises in part from the pterygomandibular raphe -- the fibrous band stretching between the pterygoid hamulus above and the posterior end of the mylohyoid line of the mandible below -- along with the medial pterygoid plate, pterygoid hamulus and mandible itself. The raphe is not attached to bone along most of its length, which is why this origin is described as a fibrous line rather than a bony ridge.
## explicit_objective
Identify the superior constrictor as the pharyngeal constrictor that originates in part from the pterygomandibular raphe.
## pitfalls
Attributing the pterygomandibular raphe origin to middle or inferior constrictor, or to stylopharyngeus/palatopharyngeus -- only superior constrictor arises from it.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-9F2F1A36419771
## label
The tympanic membrane forms the lateral wall of the middle ear cavity
## canonical_key
tympanic-membrane-lateral-wall-middle-ear
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The tympanic membrane forms almost the entire lateral wall of the middle ear (tympanic) cavity, separating it from the external acoustic meatus and transmitting sound vibrations to the ossicular chain. This lateral position is what allows otoscopic examination to visualise the membrane directly from the external ear canal, and why a perforation connects the middle ear cavity to the outside.
## explicit_objective
State that the tympanic membrane forms the lateral wall of the middle ear cavity.
## pitfalls
Placing the tympanic membrane on the medial wall (promontory/oval-round windows), floor (jugular bulb) or anterior wall (Eustachian tube opening) instead of the lateral wall.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-AEA15DF2EBDBB8
## label
The internal laryngeal nerve is purely sensory, supplying laryngeal mucosa above the vocal folds
## canonical_key
internal-laryngeal-nerve-sensation-above-vocal-folds
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The internal laryngeal nerve (the sensory branch of the superior laryngeal nerve) pierces the thyrohyoid membrane and supplies sensation to laryngeal mucosa above the vocal folds, down to their upper surface. Mucosa at and below the folds is instead supplied by the recurrent laryngeal nerve, and all intrinsic laryngeal muscles except cricothyroid (which is supplied by the motor external laryngeal nerve) are supplied by the recurrent laryngeal nerve -- the internal laryngeal nerve carries no motor fibres at all.
## explicit_objective
State that the internal laryngeal nerve is purely sensory, supplying laryngeal mucosa above the vocal folds, distinguishing it from the motor external and recurrent laryngeal nerves.
## pitfalls
Crediting the internal laryngeal nerve with motor function (cricothyroid is external laryngeal nerve territory) or with sensation below the vocal folds (recurrent laryngeal nerve territory).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-8FEF5F47B0737A
## label
Palatoglossus is the sole tongue muscle supplied by the pharyngeal plexus, not the hypoglossal nerve
## canonical_key
palatoglossus-pharyngeal-plexus
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Every tongue muscle, extrinsic and intrinsic, is supplied by the hypoglossal nerve (CN XII) except palatoglossus. Although palatoglossus acts on the tongue, it is developmentally a palatal muscle and is supplied by the pharyngeal plexus (vagal fibres, CN X) instead. This split means an isolated vagal lesion affecting the pharyngeal plexus can weaken palatoglossus specifically while every other tongue muscle, still supplied by an intact hypoglossal nerve, stays unaffected.
## explicit_objective
Identify palatoglossus as the one tongue muscle supplied by the pharyngeal plexus rather than the hypoglossal nerve.
## pitfalls
Assuming all tongue muscles share the hypoglossal nerve -- palatoglossus is the single, developmentally palatal exception.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-351C3089AC9BF8
## label
The submental triangle lies between the two anterior bellies of digastric
## canonical_key
submental-triangle-digastric-bellies
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The submental triangle is bounded by the anterior belly of digastric on each side and the hyoid bone below, with the midline symphysis menti above -- it lies directly between the two anterior digastric bellies. It contains the submental lymph nodes and small submental veins, making it a relevant landmark for floor-of-mouth and anterior tongue lymphatic drainage.
## explicit_objective
Identify the submental triangle as the neck triangle bounded by the two anterior bellies of digastric.
## pitfalls
Confusing the submental triangle with the muscular, occipital or posterior triangles, none of which are bounded by the digastric bellies.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-C7C7B035DF83B7
## label
Ptosis follows paralysis of levator palpebrae superioris's smooth-muscle part (superior tarsal/Muller's muscle), classically in Horner's syndrome
## canonical_key
ptosis-levator-palpebrae-superioris-smooth-part
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Levator palpebrae superioris has two parts: a voluntary skeletal-muscle component (oculomotor nerve, CN III) providing most eyelid elevation, and a smooth-muscle component -- the superior tarsal muscle (Muller's muscle) -- supplied by sympathetic fibres, providing the extra few millimetres of lid elevation. Loss of sympathetic supply paralyses this smooth part specifically, producing the mild ptosis classically seen in Horner's syndrome, as opposed to the complete ptosis of an oculomotor nerve palsy, which paralyses the skeletal part instead.
## explicit_objective
Identify levator palpebrae superioris's smooth-muscle part (superior tarsal/Muller's muscle, sympathetically supplied) as the structure whose paralysis produces ptosis, e.g. in Horner's syndrome.
## pitfalls
Attributing ptosis to an extraocular muscle (superior rectus/oblique) rather than to levator palpebrae superioris's own smooth-muscle component.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-F59B0633977228
## label
The stylomandibular ligament is a thickened part of the investing layer of deep cervical fascia separating the parotid and submandibular gland beds
## canonical_key
stylomandibular-ligament-deep-cervical-fascia
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The stylomandibular ligament is a discrete thickened band of the investing layer of deep cervical fascia, running from the styloid process to the angle of the mandible. This anatomical relationship is why the ligament helps keep the parotid and submandibular salivary gland beds separated, rarely allowing abscesses or tumours to spread directly between the two spaces.
## explicit_objective
Identify the stylomandibular ligament as a thickened part of the investing layer of deep cervical fascia, separating the parotid and submandibular gland beds.
## pitfalls
Confusing the stylomandibular ligament (a fascial thickening) with the thyrohyoid membrane, cricothyroid ligament, or a vertebral ligament (supraspinous) -- none of which are cervical fascia thickenings.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-EAE42F8D52C5FE
## label
Unilateral sternocleidomastoid contraction rotates the face to the opposite side while tilting the head to its own side
## canonical_key
sternocleidomastoid-head-rotation
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Each sternocleidomastoid rotates the face toward the OPPOSITE side while tilting the head toward its OWN side, and, with the extensor group, assists in extending/tilting the head upward. A single right sternocleidomastoid contraction therefore turns the face to the left and helps tip the head up. This paired, opposite-side rotation action is why testing sternocleidomastoid strength (turning the head against resistance) evaluates the accessory nerve on the side OPPOSITE to the direction the patient turns.
## explicit_objective
State that unilateral sternocleidomastoid contraction rotates the face to the OPPOSITE side (right SCM turns the face left) while tilting the head to its own side.
## pitfalls
Assuming sternocleidomastoid turns the face toward its OWN side -- it is the opposite: right SCM turns the face left, and vice versa.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-A2B4C0C672516E
## label
The great auricular nerve (cervical plexus, C2-C3) supplies skin over the angle of the mandible
## canonical_key
great-auricular-nerve-mandible-angle
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The great auricular nerve, a branch of the cervical plexus (C2, C3) that ascends over sternocleidomastoid, supplies skin over the angle of the mandible, the parotid gland region and the lower auricle. This is also why great auricular nerve injury during parotidectomy -- where it lies superficially and is at particular surgical risk -- produces numbness over the parotid region and mandibular angle rather than the mouth or upper face.
## explicit_objective
Identify the great auricular nerve (cervical plexus, C2-C3) as supplying skin over the angle of the mandible, distinguishing its territory from the trigeminal nerve's.
## pitfalls
Attributing sensation over the angle of the mandible to a trigeminal (mandibular or auriculotemporal) branch -- that specific patch is cervical plexus (great auricular nerve) territory, not trigeminal.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-64AA996B2B62AF
## label
Blood-brain barrier endothelial cells are completely surrounded by astrocyte end-feet
## canonical_key
bbb.endothelium.astrocyte-end-feet-complete-coverage
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The blood-brain barrier is formed structurally by continuous (non-fenestrated) capillary endothelial cells joined by tight junctions, sitting on a continuous basal lamina, with very few pinocytotic vesicles (limiting transcellular transport). The astrocyte foot processes (end-feet) completely ensheathe the outer surface of these capillaries, inducing and maintaining the endothelial tight-junction phenotype, though the end-feet themselves are not the diffusion barrier -- the tight-junctioned endothelium is.
## explicit_objective
State that BBB capillaries are completely surrounded by astrocyte end-feet, and distinguish this from the endothelium's own barrier features (tight junctions, continuous basal lamina, scarce pinocytotic vesicles).
## pitfalls
Assuming BBB endothelial cells have numerous pinocytotic vesicles (they have very few, unlike fenestrated capillaries elsewhere) or lie on a discontinuous basal lamina (it is continuous), or that they are joined by apical adherent junctions rather than tight junctions.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-E6FD99431C486A
## label
A lesion of the right gracile nucleus impairs proprioception of the right leg
## canonical_key
gracilenucleus.lesion.ipsilateral-leg-proprioception
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The fasciculus gracilis carries fine touch, vibration and proprioception from the lower limb and lower trunk, ascending ipsilaterally in the dorsal column without crossing until its fibres synapse in the nucleus gracilis in the closed (caudal) medulla; the second-order internal arcuate fibres then decussate to form the medial lemniscus. Because decussation happens AFTER the nucleus, a lesion at the nucleus gracilis itself produces deficits on the SAME (ipsilateral) side as the nucleus, in the leg (gracilis = lower body; the more lateral cuneate nucleus, absent below T6, serves the upper body/arm instead).
## explicit_objective
Localise a nucleus gracilis lesion to ipsilateral lower-limb proprioceptive loss, distinguishing pre-decussation (nucleus-level, ipsilateral) from post-decussation (medial lemniscus/thalamus-level, contralateral) dorsal column lesions.
## pitfalls
Applying the "dorsal column signs are contralateral" rule learned for cortical/thalamic lesions to a lesion at the nucleus itself, which is still pre-decussation and therefore ipsilateral; or confusing gracile (leg/lower body) with cuneate (arm/upper body) territory.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-5FB3373EB9059A
## label
Golgi cell dendrites in the cerebellar cortex extend through all layers of the grey matter
## canonical_key
cerebellum.golgi-cell.dendrites-all-layers
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Golgi cells are inhibitory interneurons of the cerebellar cortex granular layer. Unlike granule cells (which have short, claw-like dendrites confined to the granular layer), Golgi cell dendrites ramify widely and extend through all three layers of the cerebellar cortex grey matter (molecular, Purkinje, and granular), letting them be excited by parallel fibres in the molecular layer and in turn inhibit granule cells via feedback/feedforward loops.
## explicit_objective
State that Golgi cell dendrites span all layers of the cerebellar cortex, distinguishing this from granule cells' short intra-granular-layer dendrites and from Purkinje cells' single planar dendritic tree confined mainly to the molecular layer.
## pitfalls
Confusing Golgi cells with granule cells (short dendrites) or assuming all cerebellar interneuron dendrites are layer-restricted like the granule cell; also assuming Purkinje cells are unipolar (they are large multipolar/flask-shaped cells arranged in a single row, not unipolar).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-8C81BF3F41589B
## label
The retinal inner nuclear layer contains the cell bodies of horizontal and amacrine cells
## canonical_key
retina.inner-nuclear-layer.horizontal-amacrine-cells
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The retina's inner nuclear layer houses the cell bodies of bipolar, horizontal, amacrine and Muller (glial) cells -- of these, horizontal and amacrine cells sit at its outer and inner margins respectively, mediating lateral inhibition/modulation of the vertical photoreceptor-to-bipolar-to-ganglion signal path. This is distinct from the nerve fibre layer (unmyelinated ganglion cell axons, becoming myelinated only behind the lamina cribrosa), the inner limiting membrane (Muller cell footplate junctions, not photoreceptor junctions), and the outer plexiform layer (photoreceptor-to-bipolar synapses, not bipolar-to-ganglion).
## explicit_objective
Match retinal layers to their correct contents, specifically that the inner nuclear layer houses horizontal and amacrine cell bodies.
## pitfalls
Swapping retinal layer/content pairs -- believing the nerve fibre layer contains myelinated axons (they are unmyelinated within the globe), the inner limiting membrane forms photoreceptor-Muller junctions (it is Muller-cell footplate junctions instead), or the outer plexiform layer is where bipolar and ganglion cells synapse (that is the inner plexiform layer).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-CC7C1C358A5209
## label
Lacrimal gland acini are surrounded by myoepithelial cells
## canonical_key
lacrimalgland.acini.myoepithelial-cell-covering
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The lacrimal gland is a compound tubuloalveolar (not simple tubular) serous gland whose acini are lined by simple columnar-to-low-columnar epithelium (not pseudostratified ciliated epithelium) and are NOT mucous glands. Like other exocrine glands with a myoepithelial layer (salivary, sweat, mammary), the lacrimal acini are surrounded by contractile myoepithelial cells between the secretory epithelium and basal lamina, which contract to aid expulsion of secretion.
## explicit_objective
State that lacrimal gland acini are surrounded by myoepithelial cells, and that the gland is serous/tubuloalveolar rather than mucous or simple tubular.
## pitfalls
Assuming the lacrimal gland is a mucous gland (it is serous) or a simple tubular gland (it is compound tubuloalveolar), or that its acinar lining is pseudostratified ciliated columnar (it is simple/low columnar, non-ciliated).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-37CBA192A40FF1
## label
The palpebral conjunctiva is lined by stratified columnar epithelium with goblet cells
## canonical_key
conjunctiva.palpebral.stratified-columnar-goblet-cells
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The conjunctiva's epithelial type varies by region: the bulbar conjunctiva (over the sclera) is stratified squamous, while the palpebral conjunctiva (lining the inner eyelid) is stratified columnar epithelium with interspersed mucus-secreting goblet cells, transitioning between the two at the fornix.
## explicit_objective
State that the palpebral conjunctiva is stratified columnar epithelium with goblet cells, distinguishing it from the bulbar conjunctiva's stratified squamous epithelium.
## pitfalls
Assuming the conjunctiva has one uniform epithelial type throughout -- palpebral (stratified columnar + goblet cells) and bulbar (stratified squamous) differ by region.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-F8CD4225687128
## label
The middle ear cavity is posteriorly continuous with the mastoid air cells
## canonical_key
middleear.posterior-wall.mastoid-air-cell-continuity
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The middle ear (tympanic cavity) communicates posteriorly, via the aditus ad antrum, with the mastoid antrum and the mastoid air cell system -- the route by which middle-ear infection can spread to cause mastoiditis. This is distinct from the oval window (closed by the stapes footplate/annular ligament, not a "secondary tympanic membrane" -- that term describes the round window instead), and from the auditory (pharyngotympanic) tube, which is lined by ciliated pseudostratified/simple columnar respiratory-type epithelium, not stratified columnar. Tensor tympani attaches to the malleus, not the incus (stapedius attaches to the stapes).
## explicit_objective
State that the middle ear communicates posteriorly with the mastoid air cells via the aditus ad antrum, and correctly pair each middle-ear landmark (oval window, auditory tube epithelium, tensor tympani) with its true description.
## pitfalls
Calling the round window the "secondary tympanic membrane" and misapplying that name to the oval window; assuming the auditory tube is lined by stratified columnar epithelium (it is respiratory-type ciliated epithelium); or attaching tensor tympani to the incus instead of the malleus.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-0A73FD96D0A57F
## label
Cochlear implants are indicated when deafness is due to loss of cochlear sensory (hair) cells
## canonical_key
cochlea.cochlear-implant.hair-cell-loss-indication
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
A cochlear implant bypasses the mechanical/sensory transduction machinery of the cochlea and directly electrically stimulates the spiral ganglion afferent fibres, making it the appropriate intervention specifically for sensorineural deafness caused by loss of the cochlear sensory (hair) cells -- as opposed to isolated stereociliary or kinociliary defects, or cupula-related vestibular problems, which are different lesions.
## explicit_objective
State that cochlear implants are indicated for deafness caused by loss of cochlear (organ of Corti) sensory hair cells, since the implant substitutes for hair-cell transduction by directly stimulating the auditory nerve.
## pitfalls
Assuming a cochlear implant addresses any inner-ear problem generally, rather than specifically hair-cell loss -- a conductive hearing loss or an isolated stereocilia/kinocilium defect without hair cell loss is not the same lesion this device is designed for.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-12B9730F87F404
## label
The outer wall of the cochlear duct is formed by the stria vascularis
## canonical_key
cochlearduct.stria-vascularis.outer-wall
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The cochlear duct (scala media) is a triangular endolymph-filled space bounded by Reissner's (vestibular) membrane as its roof, the basilar membrane (bearing the organ of Corti) as its floor, and the stria vascularis -- a vascularised, ion-transporting epithelium -- as its outer wall. The stria vascularis is not passive: it actively generates the endolymph's distinctive ionic composition and the endocochlear potential. The organ of Corti sits on the basilar membrane, not the vestibular membrane, and the cochlear duct contains endolymph, not perilymph (perilymph fills the scalae vestibuli and tympani that flank it).
## explicit_objective
State that the stria vascularis forms the outer wall of the cochlear duct, and correctly place the organ of Corti on the basilar membrane (not the vestibular membrane) and endolymph (not perilymph) within the duct.
## pitfalls
Placing the organ of Corti on the vestibular (Reissner's) membrane instead of the basilar membrane, or saying the cochlear duct contains perilymph -- both flanking scalae (vestibuli and tympani) contain perilymph, while the cochlear duct itself contains endolymph.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-AA621897598B60
## label
The cupula of the crista ampullaris is a gelatinous layer of proteoglycans
## canonical_key
histology.crista-ampullaris.cupula-gelatinous-proteoglycan-layer
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The cupula is a dome-shaped, gelatinous mass composed largely of proteoglycans that caps each crista ampullaris of the semicircular ducts and embeds the stereocilia and kinocilium of the underlying hair cells, so that endolymph flow generated by angular (rotational) acceleration deflects it and bends the embedded cilia. Unlike the otolithic membrane of the maculae (utricle/saccule), which contains calcium carbonate crystals (otoconia) and responds to linear acceleration/gravity, the cupula is a thick proteoglycan membrane that lacks CaCO3 crystals -- it has no otoliths, which is exactly why it is a pure angular-acceleration (rotation) detector.
## explicit_objective
State that the cupula is a gelatinous proteoglycan structure lacking CaCO3 crystals, distinguishing it from the otoconia-bearing otolithic membrane of the maculae.
## pitfalls
Confusing the cupula (semicircular canals, angular acceleration, no otoconia) with the otolithic membrane (maculae, linear acceleration/gravity, contains CaCO3 otoconia); or assuming macular hair cells have apical motile cilia (they have stereocilia and one kinocilium, not motile cilia) or that maculae are lined by simple columnar epithelium alone rather than sensory hair cells plus columnar supporting cells.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-2C32EE28262F4E
## label
The Sheath of Henle consists of reticular fibers formed by Schwann cells around a peripheral nerve fibre
## canonical_key
peripheralnerve.sheath-of-henle.reticular-fibers-schwann-origin
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
A peripheral nerve is covered by three connective-tissue sheaths: the dense connective-tissue epineurium (outermost, encasing the whole nerve, not the perineurium), the perineurium (surrounding each fascicle, formed of flattened epithelium-like cells joined by TIGHT junctions, not gap junctions -- this is the blood-nerve barrier), and the endoneurium (surrounding individual fibres, which does NOT itself form a barrier). Within the endoneurium, the sheath of Henle is an outer layer of reticular (collagen III) fibres associated with the Schwann cells that invests each individual nerve fibre.
## explicit_objective
State that the sheath of Henle is composed of reticular fibres associated with Schwann cells, and correctly assign each peripheral-nerve connective-tissue layer (epineurium, perineurium, endoneurium) its true composition and barrier function.
## pitfalls
Calling the epineurium the "perineurium" or vice versa; assuming the perineurium's flattened cells are joined by gap junctions (they are tight junctions, forming the blood-nerve barrier); or crediting the endoneurium with forming a protective barrier (that function belongs to the perineurium).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-CEE4C1F784F4C1
## label
In the Golgi tendon (organ) spindle, the sensory nerve ending wraps around collagen bundles
## canonical_key
nerveending.golgi-tendon-organ.collagen-bundle-ending
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The Golgi tendon organ is an encapsulated proprioceptor located at the muscle-tendon junction, in series with extrafusal muscle fibres, whose sensory (group Ib) nerve ending winds around and interdigitates with collagen fibre bundles of the tendon, so that it is activated by active muscle contraction (tendon tension) more than passive stretch. This differs from free nerve endings (unencapsulated, mainly nociceptive, not mechanoreceptors for vibration/pressure -- that is Pacinian corpuscle territory), from intrafusal muscle-spindle fibres (whose central region is relatively non-striated, not striated, allowing them to be stretch-sensitive), and from Ruffini corpuscles (elongated, found in the DEEP dermis and joint capsules, not oval structures in the dermal papillae -- that location describes Meissner's corpuscles instead).
## explicit_objective
State that the Golgi tendon organ's sensory ending wraps around tendon collagen bundles, and correctly distinguish free nerve endings, intrafusal fibres and Ruffini corpuscles from this description.
## pitfalls
Assuming free nerve endings are mechanoreceptors for vibration/pressure (they are mainly nociceptors; Pacinian corpuscles serve vibration/pressure); believing intrafusal fibres have a central STRIATED nuclear region (it is relatively non-striated centrally, striated only peripherally); or placing Ruffini corpuscles in the dermal papillae of the palm (that is Meissner's corpuscle territory -- Ruffini corpuscles sit in deep dermis/joint capsules).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-792F94176AE3E6
## label
In Wallerian degeneration, digestion chambers appear in the distal part of the injured nerve fibre
## canonical_key
walleriandegeneration.distal-stump.digestion-chambers
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Wallerian degeneration is the sequence of changes in the distal stump of a transected/injured axon: the axon and myelin fragment and are phagocytosed, and Schwann cells (with recruited macrophages) form "digestion chambers" (myelin ovoids) that clear the debris and proliferate to form a guiding column (band of Bungner) for eventual axonal regrowth. Chromatolysis (dispersal of Nissl substance) and peripheral migration of the nucleus are retrograde changes in the CELL BODY (the "axon reaction"), not Wallerian degeneration itself, and silver stains demonstrate neurofibrils/axons, not myelin (osmium/Sudan stains are used for myelin).
## explicit_objective
State that digestion chambers formed by Schwann cells appear in the distal stump during Wallerian degeneration, distinguishing this distal-stump process from the cell-body's separate chromatolysis/axon-reaction response.
## pitfalls
Attributing chromatolysis or peripheral nuclear migration to Wallerian degeneration itself, when these are cell-body (axon reaction) changes rather than distal-stump events; or believing silver stain demonstrates myelin (it demonstrates neurofibrils -- myelin needs osmium/Sudan stains).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-6375D502FBE289
## label
Protoplasmic astrocytes participate in the formation of the blood-brain barrier
## canonical_key
neuroglia.protoplasmic-astrocyte.bbb-participation
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Protoplasmic astrocytes (found mainly in grey matter, with many short, thick, highly branched processes) send end-feet onto capillary walls and thereby participate in forming and maintaining the blood-brain barrier -- distinct from fibrous astrocytes (white matter, fewer/longer processes). Mesoglia (microglia) are small cells of mesodermal/monocyte lineage with scanty cytoplasm that lack centrioles and normally do NOT divide by mitosis (they are not classically pericyte-like resident dividing cells); interfascicular oligodendrocytes lie in WHITE matter (not grey matter) myelinating CNS axons; and ependymal cells line the ventricles/central canal, not "around nerve cells in ganglia" (that description fits satellite cells of peripheral ganglia instead).
## explicit_objective
State that protoplasmic astrocytes participate in blood-brain barrier formation, and correctly distinguish neuroglial cell types (protoplasmic vs fibrous astrocytes, microglia, oligodendrocytes, ependymal cells) by their true locations and functions.
## pitfalls
Confusing protoplasmic (grey matter) with fibrous (white matter) astrocytes; assuming microglia have centrioles and readily divide, or that interfascicular oligodendrocytes are found in grey matter (they myelinate white matter tracts); or placing ependymal cells around neurons in ganglia (that is satellite cells' location, not ependymal cells').

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-FEBC0C7946940F
## label
The pterygopalatine ganglion relays parasympathetic secretomotor fibres to the lacrimal gland
## canonical_key
pterygopalatineganglion.function.lacrimal-secretomotor
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The pterygopalatine ganglion is the parasympathetic relay station for the lacrimal gland: preganglionic fibres travel in the facial nerve, greater petrosal nerve and nerve of the pterygoid canal to synapse here, and postganglionic fibres reach the lacrimal gland via the zygomatic and lacrimal nerves. Interruption of this pathway reduces tear secretion. This distinguishes it from the otic ganglion (parotid) and ciliary ganglion (pupil/accommodation).
## explicit_objective
Identify the pterygopalatine ganglion as the parasympathetic relay for lacrimal secretomotor fibres, distinguishing it from the otic and ciliary ganglia.
## pitfalls
Confusing the parasympathetic secretomotor supply (pterygopalatine ganglion) with the sympathetic vasomotor supply (superior cervical ganglion) that merely hitch-hikes to the gland.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-DC8FBB18182F1A
## label
The transverse cervical nerve crosses horizontally over sternocleidomastoid
## canonical_key
transversecervicalnerve.course.crosses-sternocleidomastoid
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The transverse (anterior) cutaneous nerve of the neck is a cutaneous branch of the cervical plexus (C2-C3). It winds around the posterior border of sternocleidomastoid near its midpoint and runs horizontally (transversely) forward across the muscle to supply skin of the anterior triangle. This transverse course distinguishes it from the other cervical plexus cutaneous branches.
## explicit_objective
Identify the transverse cervical nerve as the cervical plexus branch crossing horizontally over sternocleidomastoid.
## pitfalls
Confusing it with the great auricular nerve, which ascends vertically over the same muscle toward the ear.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-2914B44330D1B4
## label
A complete lateral cervical (branchial) fistula arises from the second pharyngeal pouch and cervical sinus
## canonical_key
branchialfistula.embryology.second-pharyngeal-pouch-cervical-sinus
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
A complete lateral cervical (branchial) fistula runs from an external opening at the anterior border of sternocleidomastoid to an internal opening in the tonsillar fossa. It forms when the second pharyngeal pouch (giving the internal tonsillar-fossa opening) persists together with the cervical sinus (the buried ectodermal-groove remnant, giving the external opening). Persistence of both is what makes it a through-and-through fistula rather than a blind cyst.
## explicit_objective
Explain that a complete branchial fistula ending at the tonsillar fossa involves the second pharyngeal pouch and the cervical sinus.
## pitfalls
Attributing an internal opening at the tonsillar fossa to the third pouch (which opens near the piriform fossa) or forgetting that a complete fistula needs both pouch and cervical-sinus components.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-A14AEEDF434B4D
## label
Cleft lip results from failure of fusion of the maxillary prominence with the intermaxillary segment
## canonical_key
cleftlip.embryology.maxillary-intermaxillary-fusion-failure
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The upper lip forms when the maxillary prominence of each side fuses with the intermaxillary segment (the merged medial nasal prominences, which contribute the philtrum and the incisor-bearing premaxilla). Failure of the maxillary prominence to fuse with the intermaxillary segment leaves the gap of a lateral cleft lip. This maxillary-to-intermaxillary fusion is the step that closes the upper lip.
## explicit_objective
State that cleft lip results from failure of fusion of the maxillary prominence with the intermaxillary segment.
## pitfalls
Confusing lateral cleft lip (maxillary-intermaxillary failure) with the rare median cleft (medial nasal prominences not merging) or oblique facial cleft (lateral-nasal to medial-nasal failure).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-B51FF245ADF2D1
## label
General somatic sensation is localised to the primary somatosensory cortex (areas 3, 1, 2)
## canonical_key
somatosensorycortex.localization.areas-3-1-2-general-sensation
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Brodmann areas 3, 1 and 2 form the primary somatosensory cortex in the postcentral gyrus, the cortical destination for general somatic sensation (touch, proprioception, pain and temperature discrimination) from the contralateral body half. A lesion here causes contralateral loss of general sensation. Its somatotopic sensory homunculus mirrors the primary motor cortex across the central sulcus.
## explicit_objective
Localise general somatic sensation to the primary somatosensory cortex (areas 3, 1, 2) in the postcentral gyrus.
## pitfalls
Confusing the primary somatosensory area (3,1,2) with the primary visual (17), auditory (41,42) or language-association (22) areas.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-D33D684EE10AEE
## label
The paracentral lobule contains the cortical control of the bladder and bowel sphincters
## canonical_key
paracentrallobule.function.bladder-bowel-cortical-control
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The paracentral lobule, on the medial surface of the hemisphere astride the central sulcus, contains the lower-limb representation and the higher-centre control of the bladder and rectal (anal) sphincters. A lesion here (e.g. parasagittal meningioma or anterior cerebral artery infarct) produces urinary and faecal incontinence, often with lower-limb weakness. Its medial, midline location is why sphincter control is vulnerable to parasagittal lesions.
## explicit_objective
Identify the paracentral lobule as the cortical region controlling the bladder and bowel sphincters, whose lesion causes incontinence.
## pitfalls
Attributing incontinence to the premotor or parietal association areas rather than the medial-surface paracentral lobule.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-F70E1D7CCAB7A2
## label
Broca's area (areas 44, 45) is the motor speech area of the dominant hemisphere
## canonical_key
brocasarea.function.motor-speech-expression
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Broca's area (Brodmann areas 44 and 45 in the inferior frontal gyrus of the dominant hemisphere) is the motor speech area that programmes the articulatory movements of speech. A lesion causes expressive (non-fluent, Broca's) aphasia: comprehension is intact but fluent speech output is lost. Its position just anterior to the motor face/larynx representation links it to speech output.
## explicit_objective
Localise motor (expressive) speech to Broca's area (areas 44, 45) in the dominant inferior frontal gyrus.
## pitfalls
Confusing Broca's (motor/expressive) area with Wernicke's area (22, sensory/receptive language) or the premotor area.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-54686EA08F458E
## label
Colour discrimination is a function of the visual association cortex (areas 18, 19)
## canonical_key
visualassociationcortex.function.areas-18-19-colour-discrimination
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Areas 18 and 19 are the visual association cortex, where the raw visual input from primary visual cortex (area 17) is interpreted, including higher-order processing of colour, form and movement. A lesion here can impair colour discrimination (a visual agnosia) while basic sight is preserved. This distinguishes association cortex (interpretation) from primary visual cortex (detection).
## explicit_objective
Attribute higher-order colour discrimination to the visual association cortex (areas 18, 19), not the primary visual cortex.
## pitfalls
Assuming loss of colour discrimination reflects an area 17 (primary visual) lesion, which instead causes a field defect.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-F64784AD391EED
## label
Skilled voluntary (fine hand) movement is executed by the primary motor cortex (area 4)
## canonical_key
primarymotorcortex.localization.area-4
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Brodmann area 4, the primary motor cortex in the precentral gyrus, executes voluntary movement of the contralateral body, with a disproportionately large cortical representation for the hand and fingers. A lesion weakens precise skilled finger movements. Its large motor-homunculus hand area is why fine manual dexterity is heavily affected by lesions here.
## explicit_objective
Localise execution of skilled voluntary (especially fine hand) movement to the primary motor cortex, Brodmann area 4.
## pitfalls
Attributing loss of skilled hand work to parietal association areas (40,39) rather than the primary motor cortex.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-B593873E1E2C06
## label
The fourth ventricle lies between the pons/medulla and the cerebellum
## canonical_key
fourthventricle.location.between-pons-cerebellum
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The fourth ventricle is the tent-shaped hindbrain (rhombencephalic) cavity, with the pons and upper medulla forming its floor (rhomboid fossa) in front and the cerebellum forming its roof behind. It communicates above with the cerebral aqueduct and below with the central canal. Its CSF escapes into the subarachnoid space through the median and lateral apertures in its roof.
## explicit_objective
State that the fourth ventricle lies between the pons/medulla and the cerebellum.
## pitfalls
Confusing the fourth ventricle with the third ventricle (diencephalon) or placing a cavity inside the cerebellum itself.

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The pia mater is the innermost, delicate vascular meningeal layer, closely investing the brain surface and dipping into every sulcus and fissure, following the contours of gyri and sulci exactly. Unlike the arachnoid (which bridges over sulci), the pia cannot be stripped off the cortex. This distinguishes it from the tough dura and the sulcus-bridging arachnoid.
## explicit_objective
State that the pia mater follows the brain surface into all its sulci, unlike the arachnoid and dura.
## pitfalls
Swapping the pia (enters all sulci) with the arachnoid (bridges over sulci, leaving cisterns beneath).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-F3B523893E3788
## label
The cisterna magna is the cerebellomedullary cistern
## canonical_key
cisternamagna.location.cerebellomedullary-cistern
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The cisterna magna is the anatomical name of the cerebellomedullary cistern, the large subarachnoid space between the undersurface of the cerebellum and the posterior surface of the medulla oblongata. It receives CSF from the fourth ventricle through the median aperture (foramen of Magendie) and is a site for cisternal puncture. Its large accessible size earns it the name magna (great cistern).
## explicit_objective
Identify the cisterna magna as the cerebellomedullary cistern, between the cerebellum and medulla.
## pitfalls
Confusing the cisterna magna with the pontine, interpeduncular or lateral-fissure cisterns.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-ABD88AE9E58D86
## label
The metathalamus (geniculate bodies) is attached to the posterior part of the thalamus
## canonical_key
metathalamus.location.posterior-thalamus-geniculate-bodies
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The metathalamus consists of the medial and lateral geniculate bodies, which project from the posterior (pulvinar) region of the thalamus. The lateral geniculate body is the visual relay and the medial geniculate body the auditory relay. Their posterior inferolateral attachment to the pulvinar makes the metathalamus the diencephalic part attached to the posterior thalamus.
## explicit_objective
Identify the metathalamus (medial and lateral geniculate bodies) as the diencephalic part attached to the posterior thalamus.
## pitfalls
Confusing the metathalamus with the epithalamus (pineal/habenula), hypothalamus (below/anterior) or subthalamus (below).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-87E6D9E11F2656
## label
The optic radiation runs in the retrolenticular part of the internal capsule
## canonical_key
internalcapsule.retrolenticular.optic-radiation
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The retrolenticular part of the internal capsule lies behind the lentiform nucleus and carries the optic radiation (geniculocalcarine fibres) from the lateral geniculate body to the primary visual cortex, along with posterior thalamic radiation fibres. A retrolenticular lesion produces a contralateral visual field defect. Its position behind the lentiform nucleus distinguishes it from the sublenticular part below.
## explicit_objective
State that the optic radiation runs in the retrolenticular part of the internal capsule.
## pitfalls
Confusing the retrolenticular part (optic radiation) with the posterior limb (corticospinal/sensory) or sublenticular part (auditory radiation).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-8BEEDA00FFCCEE
## label
The corpus callosum is the commissure connecting corresponding areas of the two hemispheres
## canonical_key
corpuscallosum.function.commissural-fibres-hemispheres
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The corpus callosum is the largest commissure of the brain, a broad plate of commissural fibres connecting corresponding (homologous) cortical areas of the two cerebral hemispheres across the midline. Commissural fibres by definition join matching regions on the two sides. Callosal lesions produce disconnection syndromes between the two sides of the brain.
## explicit_objective
Classify the corpus callosum as the commissural bundle connecting corresponding areas of the two hemispheres, distinct from projection and association fibres.
## pitfalls
Confusing commissural fibres (corpus callosum, across hemispheres) with association fibres (cingulum, superior longitudinal fasciculus, within one hemisphere) or projection fibres (vertical).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-2FF114258317D6
## label
The lateral ventricles lie within the cerebral hemispheres
## canonical_key
lateralventricles.location.cerebral-hemispheres
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Each lateral ventricle is a C-shaped cavity lying entirely within one cerebral hemisphere, with a body and anterior (frontal), posterior (occipital) and inferior (temporal) horns extending into the lobes. They communicate with the midline third ventricle through the interventricular foramina (of Monro). Their sweeping course through all the lobes makes them the ventricles of the cerebral hemispheres.
## explicit_objective
State that the lateral ventricles lie within the cerebral hemispheres and communicate with the third ventricle via the interventricular foramina.
## pitfalls
Misplacing the lateral ventricles in the diencephalon (that is the third ventricle) or brainstem/cerebellum.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-94C3FA287AE06E
## label
The trochlear nerve is the only cranial nerve emerging from the dorsal brainstem
## canonical_key
trochlearnerve.attachment.dorsal-brainstem
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The trochlear nerve (CN IV) is the only cranial nerve to emerge from the posterior (dorsal) surface of the brainstem, appearing just below the inferior colliculi before curving around the midbrain. It is also the only cranial nerve whose fibres decussate before exiting, and the most slender cranial nerve. This dorsal exit makes it vulnerable in the ambient cistern.
## explicit_objective
State that the trochlear nerve (CN IV) is the only cranial nerve emerging from the dorsal (posterior) surface of the brainstem.
## pitfalls
Assuming all cranial nerves emerge ventrally; the trochlear nerve is the dorsal-exit exception.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-C2F2FC6CAD1FE8
## label
The choroid plexus of the third ventricle lies in its roof
## canonical_key
thirdventricle.choroidplexus.roof
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The choroid plexus of the third ventricle lies in its roof, formed by the tela choroidea (a double fold of pia) invaginating the ependymal roof between the two thalami, continuous through the interventricular foramina with the plexus of the lateral ventricles. The third ventricle communicates with the lateral ventricles via the interventricular foramina and with the fourth ventricle via the cerebral aqueduct. Its plexus contributes to CSF production.
## explicit_objective
State that the third ventricle's choroid plexus lies in its roof, and correctly assign its two communications.
## pitfalls
Swapping the third ventricle's communications: interventricular foramina go to the lateral ventricles, the cerebral aqueduct to the fourth ventricle.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-A2A8A85067BDE0
## label
The middle cerebral artery supplies the lateral surface including the auditory area
## canonical_key
middlecerebralartery.territory.auditory-area
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The middle cerebral artery supplies most of the lateral surface of the cerebral hemisphere, including the auditory area (Brodmann areas 41 and 42) in the superior temporal gyrus, and the face-and-upper-limb motor/sensory cortex and cortical language areas. The lower-limb motor/sensory representation on the medial surface is anterior cerebral artery territory instead. This broad lateral distribution defines MCA occlusion syndromes.
## explicit_objective
Recognise that the MCA supplies the lateral surface including the auditory area, distinguishing MCA from anterior cerebral artery territory.
## pitfalls
Assuming the MCA supplies the whole-body motor/sensory strip; the medial (lower-limb) part is anterior cerebral artery.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-FBA861A98E005E
## label
The corpora quadrigemina form the posterior (dorsal) surface of the midbrain
## canonical_key
midbrain.posteriorsurface.corpora-quadrigemina
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The posterior (dorsal) surface of the midbrain is the tectum, formed by the corpora quadrigemina — the paired superior and inferior colliculi. The superior colliculi are visual reflex centres and the inferior colliculi auditory relay centres, and the trochlear nerves emerge just below the inferior colliculi. The cerebral peduncles form the anterior (ventral) surface instead.
## explicit_objective
State that the corpora quadrigemina (tectum) form the posterior surface of the midbrain, the cerebral peduncles the anterior surface.
## pitfalls
Placing the fourth ventricle (a hindbrain cavity) or the cerebral peduncles (ventral) on the posterior midbrain surface.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-60895CDC74C11A
## label
The posterolateral fissure separates the flocculonodular lobe from the posterior lobe
## canonical_key
posterolateralfissure.cerebellum.separates-flocculonodular-posterior-lobe
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The posterolateral fissure separates the flocculonodular lobe (the phylogenetically oldest, vestibular part of the cerebellum) from the posterior lobe. It is the first cerebellar fissure to appear in development, consistent with the flocculonodular lobe being the oldest part. Recognising it defines the three-lobe division of the cerebellum (anterior, posterior, flocculonodular).
## explicit_objective
Identify the posterolateral fissure as separating the flocculonodular lobe from the posterior lobe of the cerebellum.
## pitfalls
Confusing the posterolateral fissure with the primary fissure (anterior vs posterior lobe) or the horizontal fissure.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-29D4FC563514E5
## label
The superficial middle cerebral vein runs in the lateral sulcus and drains to the cavernous sinus
## canonical_key
superficialmiddlecerebralvein.course.posterior-ramus-lateral-sulcus
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The superficial middle cerebral vein runs along the posterior ramus of the lateral sulcus (Sylvian fissure), draining the cortex around the fissure, then passes forward to end in the cavernous sinus (via the sphenoparietal sinus). It communicates with the transverse sinus through the inferior anastomotic vein (of Labbe) and with the superior sagittal sinus through the superior anastomotic vein (of Trolard). Its course within the lateral sulcus defines it.
## explicit_objective
State that the superficial middle cerebral vein runs in the lateral sulcus and drains chiefly to the cavernous sinus.
## pitfalls
Assuming it ends directly in the transverse or superior sagittal sinus; those are reached only indirectly via the anastomotic veins.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-52B22D62663877
## label
The arteria radicularis magna (of Adamkiewicz) supplies the lumbar enlargement of the spinal cord
## canonical_key
arteriaradicularismagna.territory.lumbar-enlargement-adamkiewicz
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The arteria radicularis magna (great anterior radicular artery, or artery of Adamkiewicz) is the dominant segmental feeder reinforcing the anterior spinal artery in the lower thoracic and lumbar region, and the principal supply to the lumbar (lumbosacral) enlargement of the spinal cord. Because it supplies so much of the lower cord, its interruption risks infarction and anterior spinal artery syndrome. The thin anterior spinal artery alone is inadequate there.
## explicit_objective
Identify the arteria radicularis magna (artery of Adamkiewicz) as the dominant arterial supply to the lumbar enlargement.
## pitfalls
Crediting the anterior spinal artery in general rather than the specific great radicular artery that reinforces it in the lumbar region.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-EE843B5CFCB6A6
## label
Presynaptic inhibition works by hyperpolarising the presynaptic terminal, cutting Ca2+ entry and transmitter release
## canonical_key
synapse.presynaptic-inhibition.gaba-hyperpolarization-primary-afferent
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Presynaptic inhibition is mediated by an axo-axonic synapse in which an inhibitory (usually GABAergic) interneuron acts on the presynaptic terminal of an excitatory neuron. By hyperpolarising and reducing the depolarisation of that terminal (via increased K+ and Cl- conductance), it lowers voltage-gated Ca2+ channel opening when the action potential arrives, so less transmitter is released. This selectively damps one input without changing the postsynaptic cell's overall excitability. It differs from postsynaptic inhibition (the IPSP), which acts on the receiving membrane itself.
## explicit_objective
Explain presynaptic inhibition as reduced transmitter release from a hyperpolarised presynaptic terminal at an axo-axonic (GABAergic) synapse.
## pitfalls
Confusing presynaptic inhibition (acts on the transmitting terminal, reducing Ca2+ entry) with postsynaptic inhibition/IPSP (acts on the receiving membrane).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-ED81318B16C20C
## label
Chemical synaptic transmission is metabolically demanding and is depressed by hypoglycaemia, hypoxia and acidosis
## canonical_key
synapse.transmission.sensitive-to-hypoxia-hypercapnia-acidosis
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Chemical synapses have several special properties: one-way (Dale) conduction, synaptic delay, fatigue on repetitive stimulation, and marked sensitivity to the internal chemical environment. Because transmitter synthesis, vesicle recycling and ion pumping are energy-dependent, synaptic transmission is readily depressed by hypoxia, hypoglycaemia and acidosis, and modulated by drugs. Alkalosis raises neuronal excitability while acidosis and hypoxia depress it, which is why these metabolic disturbances rapidly alter consciousness. This metabolic vulnerability is greatest at the synapse rather than along the axon.
## explicit_objective
List the special properties of chemical synapses and identify hypoglycaemia, hypoxia and acidosis as factors that depress synaptic transmission.
## pitfalls
Assuming presynaptic terminals release only excitatory transmitters, or that Ca2+ channels sit on the postsynaptic membrane — the voltage-gated Ca2+ channels are presynaptic.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-16EB6178F5D48C
## label
Neurotransmitter release requires Ca2+ influx into the presynaptic terminal through voltage-gated channels
## canonical_key
synapse.transmitter-release.presynaptic-calcium-influx
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
When an action potential reaches the presynaptic terminal it depolarises the membrane and opens voltage-gated Ca2+ channels, allowing Ca2+ to flow into the terminal. The rise in intracellular Ca2+ triggers vesicle fusion with the presynaptic membrane and exocytosis of transmitter into the synaptic cleft. Release is therefore Ca2+-dependent, graded with Ca2+ entry, and abolished if extracellular Ca2+ is removed or the channels are blocked. Neither acetylcholinesterase synthesis nor terminal hyperpolarisation drives release — those would oppose it.
## explicit_objective
Identify presynaptic Ca2+ influx through voltage-gated channels as the trigger for neurotransmitter release at a central chemical synapse.
## pitfalls
Thinking release depends on ligand-gated Ca2+ channels or on acetylcholinesterase — release depends on VOLTAGE-gated Ca2+ channels in the presynaptic terminal.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-79F78F65CC5CA9
## label
An IPSP hyperpolarises the postsynaptic membrane, moving it away from the firing threshold
## canonical_key
synapse.ipsp.moves-membrane-away-from-firing-threshold
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
An inhibitory postsynaptic potential (IPSP) is a graded hyperpolarisation of the postsynaptic membrane produced by inhibitory transmitters (e.g. GABA, glycine) that open Cl- channels (increasing Cl- conductance/influx) or K+ channels (increasing K+ efflux). Summating with excitatory inputs, it moves the membrane potential further from — or opposes movement toward — the firing threshold, reducing the probability of an action potential. IPSPs interact algebraically with EPSPs on the postsynaptic neuron, so the cell fires only if net depolarisation reaches threshold. Increased (not decreased) Cl- conductance and decreased Na+ conductance underlie the hyperpolarisation.
## explicit_objective
Define the IPSP as a hyperpolarising postsynaptic potential that summates with other inputs and moves the membrane away from threshold.
## pitfalls
Stating the IPSP is produced by DECREASED Cl- conductance or by increased Na+ conductance — it is produced by INCREASED Cl- (or K+) conductance; increased Na+ conductance is depolarising (an EPSP).

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-667B888DA911EB
## label
Post-tetanic potentiation is caused by residual Ca2+ accumulating in the presynaptic terminal after a tetanus
## canonical_key
synapse.post-tetanic-potentiation.presynaptic-calcium-accumulation
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Post-tetanic potentiation is a short-term increase in synaptic strength: after a rapid train (tetanus) of presynaptic stimuli, subsequent single stimuli produce larger postsynaptic responses for seconds to minutes. The mechanism is accumulation of residual Ca2+ in the presynaptic terminal, because Ca2+ enters faster during the tetanus than it can be pumped out, so more transmitter is released per impulse afterward. It is a presynaptic phenomenon and a simple form of synaptic plasticity/short-term memory. It is distinct from long-term potentiation, which involves postsynaptic NMDA/AMPA receptor changes.
## explicit_objective
Attribute post-tetanic potentiation to residual Ca2+ accumulation in the presynaptic terminal after high-frequency stimulation.
## pitfalls
Ascribing post-tetanic potentiation to NMDA/AMPA activation (that is LTP) or to a third inhibitory/excitatory neuron — PTP is presynaptic Ca2+ accumulation.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-B75379027065F0
## label
Convergence in a neuronal pool lets one neuron integrate many inputs and select important signals
## canonical_key
neuronalpool.convergence.signal-selection-integration
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Convergence is the arrangement in which many presynaptic fibres, from one source or several sources, terminate on a single postsynaptic neuron. It allows spatial summation of inputs so that a neuron integrates information from multiple sources and can select important signals while ignoring unimportant ones. This is the opposite of divergence, where one neuron distributes signals to many. Convergence from multiple sources is the basis of the integrative, decision-making function of neuronal pools.
## explicit_objective
Define convergence as many inputs onto one neuron, enabling integration and selection of important signals.
## pitfalls
Swapping convergence with divergence — 'one neuron stimulating many' and 'distribution of signals' describe divergence, not convergence.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-BD7982591CF0AE
## label
Facilitation arises when two spaced inputs' subliminal fringes overlap, summating to discharge fringe neurons
## canonical_key
neuronalpool.facilitation.subliminal-fringe-overlap
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
In a neuronal pool each input fibre has a central discharge (liminal) zone of neurons it excites to threshold, surrounded by a facilitated (subliminal fringe) zone brought only partway to threshold. When two input fibres are stimulated far enough apart that their discharge zones do not overlap but their subliminal fringes do, the subthreshold EPSPs summate and additional fringe neurons now reach threshold — so the combined response exceeds the sum of the separate responses. This spatial-summation gain is facilitation. When inputs are close together their discharge zones overlap and the combined response is LESS than the sum (occlusion).
## explicit_objective
Explain facilitation as overlap of the subliminal fringes of two spaced inputs, contrasting it with occlusion when inputs are close.
## pitfalls
Reversing facilitation and occlusion: facilitation needs the two inputs SPACED APART (fringes overlap); inputs close together give occlusion, with fewer impulses than the sum.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-331FB77B7D330B
## label
The receptor (generator) potential is a graded, non-propagated potential that outlasts the action potential
## canonical_key
receptorpotential.property.duration-outlasts-action-potential
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
A receptor (generator) potential is the local, graded depolarisation a sensory receptor produces when a stimulus is transduced. It is non-propagated, has an amplitude graded with stimulus strength, and its duration is longer than that of the action potential it may trigger — it does not have the fixed all-or-none time course or the long absolute refractory period of an action potential. If it reaches threshold at the first node/spike-initiating zone it generates one or more action potentials, but by itself it does not always fire a spike. Its longer duration is what allows repetitive firing during a maintained stimulus.
## explicit_objective
State that the receptor potential is graded and non-propagated and that its duration outlasts the action potential.
## pitfalls
Believing the receptor potential is all-or-none, always fires a spike, or has a long absolute refractory period — those are action-potential properties.

# Item
## id
CON-NEU-56947438931051
## label
Sensory transduction converts stimulus energy into a local, graded depolarisation of the receptor
## canonical_key
sensoryreceptor.transduction.stimulus-energy-to-local-depolarization
## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
A sensory receptor transduces its adequate stimulus (mechanical, thermal, chemical, photic) into a local graded depolarisation — the receptor potential — by opening ion channels in the receptor membrane. This potential is graded and non-propagating (it spreads electrotonically, not self-propagating like an action potential), and each receptor is specialised (has a low threshold) for one modality of energy, its adequate stimulus, though very strong energy of another form can also excite it. The frequency of action potentials generated rises with stimulus strength but not in a simple linear doubling relationship — it follows a power/logarithmic function. Thus stimulus energy is first converted into a local depolarisation before impulses are generated.
## explicit_objective
Describe sensory transduction as conversion of stimulus energy into a local graded depolarisation, with receptors tuned to an adequate stimulus.
## pitfalls
Calling the receptor potential 'self-propagating' or assuming impulse frequency doubles when stimulus strength doubles (the relationship is a power function, not linear).
